For in this fiery *solvent* <term>Solvente</term> there are no such unequal parts as in the *corrosive* ones, but this fiery *solvent* is of an entirely uniform and simple essence, and knows no other separability than that it either remains entirely with the dissolved substance as a *fixed salt* <term>fixes Saltz: a stable substance that resists heat and does not evaporate</term>, or rises up entirely with it as a *volatile spirit* <term>flüchtiger Geist: a substance that easily evaporates or turns to vapor with heat</term>. 

Therefore, that which is drawn off from the *dissolved matter* after the *dissolution* has occurred is not changed in the least from its former strength and power which it possessed before it performed the *dissolution*. Also, let no one wonder that a part of the *solvent* <term>solventis</term> remains here with the *dissolved matter*, for this is brought about by the law of such *unity of essence* <note>original: Einwesigkeit; a state where two things share the same inner nature or "oneness"</note>, where like holds onto its like with great desire and unites itself indissolubly with it. # Of the Philosophers' Sulfur

A corrosive <note>referring to common mineral acids like aqua fortis</note> can always be separated again from the solute <note>the substance that has been dissolved</note> through art, but the essential *solvent* <term>solvens: the active substance that performs the dissolving</term> cannot. Therefore our philosopher <note>likely referring to Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a pioneer of chemistry who wrote extensively on the "Alcahest"</note> also confesses, in this sense, regarding the *Alcahest* <term>Alcahest: a legendary "universal solvent" sought by alchemists, said to reduce any body to its primary liquid state</term> itself, that it is held by its own kind and transformed into another nature; for his words regarding this in *The Unknown Action of Governance* <note>original: "ignora actione regiminis," likely referring to Van Helmont's treatise "De Ignota Actione Regiminis"</note> paragraph 11 read thus: "The liquor Alcahest perfectly reduces all tangible bodies of the whole universe into their first life <note>original: "vitam eorundem primam"; the primary, uncorrupted state of matter</note>, without any change to itself or diminution of its powers; however, by its equal alone is it drawn under the yoke <note>original: "subter jugum trahitur"; an idiom meaning to be mastered or brought into union</note> and changed." For this reason, the ancient sages also said: "Nature rejoices in nature, nature conquers nature, nature transforms nature," <note>original: "Natura naturâ gaudet, natura naturam vincit, permutat"</note> etc. And all of nature and its powers testify to this: that like is always inclined to remain with its like. Indeed, in this very thing consists all growth that occurs in nature. It is not my purpose here to elaborate further on this, but I must only point to it as if with a finger, by the opportunity of our nature-compliant *dissolution* <term>solution: the process of extracting the essence of a substance</term> of the *soul* <term>animae: the "soul" or inner flammable principle (Sulfur) of a metal</term> from copper. This soul, as a true fire, prepares the fiery *solvent* <term>solvens</term> from the *alkali* <term>alcali: a salt derived from plant ashes or minerals</term>, holds it to itself as its equal, and is inclined to remain inseparably with it. Now someone might say: because the tincture of copper, as the element of fire, stands entirely turned outward in this volatile and well-prepared *crocus* <term>croco: "Crocus of Venus," a fine reddish-orange powder made from copper</term>, why then can it not be [fixed] by a fixed *alkali*, which is indeed also very fiery and of the same nature as the soul of the

### Of the Sulphur of the Philosophers

...[A] corrosive <note>referring to common mineral acids like aqua fortis</note> can always be separated from the dissolved substance again through art <note>the "art" refers to alchemical techniques like distillation</note>, but the essential **solvent** <term>solvens: the active liquid agent that performs the dissolving</term> cannot. Therefore our philosopher <note>likely Jan Baptist van Helmont, a famous 17th-century chemist</note>, in such a sense, himself confesses regarding the liquid **Alcahest** <term>Alcahest: a legendary universal solvent capable of reducing any body to its primary matter without being consumed</term> that it is held by its own kind and transformed into another nature, as his words regarding this in *The Unknown Action of Governance* <note>original: "ignota actione regiminis"</note> section 11 state: "The liquid Alcahest perfectly reduces all tangible bodies of the whole universe into their first life, without any mutation of itself or diminution of its powers; however, by its own peer alone is it drawn under the yoke and changed." <note>original: "Liquor Alcahest omnia totius universi corpora tangibilia perfectè reducit in vitam eorundem primam, absque ulla sui mutatione, viriumque diminutione, à solo autem suo compari subter jugum trahitur atque permutatur."</note>

Therefore the ancient sages also said: "Nature rejoices in nature, nature overcomes nature, nature changes nature, etc." <note>original: "Natura naturâ gaudet, natura naturam vincit, permutat, &c."; a foundational axiom of alchemy attributed to Pseudo-Democritus</note> And all of nature and all its powers testify to this: that like is always inclined to remain with its like. Indeed, in this very thing consists all growth that occurs in nature. It is not my intention to elaborate further on this here, but I must only point it out as if with a finger, by the opportunity of our nature-compliant solution of the **soul** <term>animæ: the "soul" or essential life-force of a substance</term> from **copper**. This [soul], being a true fire, prepares the fiery **solvent** out of the **alkali** <term>alcali: a basic salt used here as the base for the solvent</term>, holds it to itself as its own peer, and is inclined to remain inseparably with it. 

Now someone might ask: since the **tincture** <term>tinctur: the concentrated essence or medicinal "color" of a metal</term> of copper—as the element of fire—stands completely turned outward in this volatile and well-prepared **saffron** <term>croco: "crocus," a term for a fine metallic powder or oxide, usually reddish in color</term>, why can it then not be [taken] by a fixed alkali, which is indeed also very fiery and of the same nature as the soul of the...

<summary>The author explains that the "Alcahest" or universal solvent is unique because it cannot be separated from its solute once they bond, due to their shared "fiery" nature. This principle of "like attracting like" is presented as the secret behind the growth of all things in nature and the extraction of the "soul" of copper.</summary>

If you, however, mix such an **alkali** <term>alcali: a caustic, salty substance used as a solvent</term> with this **saffron** <term>croco: "crocus cupri," a reddish powder or oxide of copper</term>, rub them together, and undertake this separation by way of the "dry path" <note>The "dry path" refers to alchemical processes using high heat and crucibles rather than liquid solutions.</note> through fire, you will achieve only this much: because the fieriness of the alkali is strengthened and made much greater by the external fire than it was by nature, and the prepared saffron is further opened by the same fire and made more suitable for extraction, the alkali does indeed seize and separate a portion of the **tincture** <term>Tinctur: the essential soul or coloring principle of a metal</term>.

Thus, you obtain coarse, material copper once again, and in this way all your previous difficult labor is brought to naught. 

The small amount that the alkali has taken into itself is indeed the true **Sulphur** <term>Sulphur: not common sulfur, but the internal, combustible "soul" of the metal</term> and golden tincture of copper; for if this alkali is rubbed onto a bit of silver with some moisture, it coats the silver most beautifully with a golden color, which is a delight to behold. But there is so little of it that it must rightly be valued more as a shadow than as the true essence of the tincture.

This can also quite easily happen again from this small amount of extracted **sulphur of copper** <note>original: *Sulphure cupri*; the "sulphur" refers to the internal, combustible, and coloring principle of the metal</note>, if one melts it in water and drips a **corrosive** <term>corrosive: a sharp acid or reactive agent used to precipitate substances</term> into it; then the alkali unites with the corrosive and lets the small amount of extracted sulphur fall to the bottom, which after drying is a powder.

Where would our **philosopher's teaching** <note>original: *Philoſophi Lehre*; likely referring to the established authorities of alchemy like Hermes or Paracelsus</note> remain then? He maintains that after this golden **soul** <term>anima: the vitalizing, spiritual essence of a substance</term> has been separated from the body, it should be **coagulated** <term>coagulated: thickened or solidified from a liquid state</term> and **fixed** <term>fixed: rendered stable and resistant to fire</term>. But how can it be coagulated if it has not been turned into a moisture by an **essential solvent** <term>eſſentialiſches Solvens: a powerful medium capable of dissolving matter into its primary elements</term>? That is to say, it must be softened in such a way that it remains an oil or a fluid, penetrating, spiritual essence after its separation from the body.

This is the true hallmark: that this golden soul has been essentially and naturally unlocked from its innermost center. But a coarse material alkali cannot provide such glory, as can be seen from the small amount of sulphur that fell to the bottom—although such sulphur still possesses its own excellent virtues. # Of the Sulphur of the Philosophers

...of **copper** <note>original: *cupri*</note> having been taken and ground quite small, and if one were to pour a highly rectified **spirit of wine** <term>Spiritus vini: a concentrated alcohol used as a solvent; the author considers it too "watery" for high alchemy</term> over it, and set it in the heat to be extracted, [one might think] that in such a way the extracted **tincture** <term>tincture: the concentrated color or "soul" extracted from a metal</term> of the copper would pass into the spirit of wine, and thus through and from the same acquire an essential moisture, and could be transformed into a penetrating oil; no, far from it.

The spirit of wine is much too weak and powerless for this; it is, after all, for the most part only common water, and from an entire pound of the strongest spirit of wine, not much more than one **loth** <term>loth: a historical German unit of weight, roughly 14 to 15 grams</term> of essential salt can be made. How then should such a scattered, widely dispersed power be able to **radically** <note>original: *radicaliter*; meaning to the very root or fundamental nature of the substance</note> soften this fiery, concentrated essence from the copper? There is no equality between these two at all; rather, a concentrated, spiritual, penetrating fire is required for this, which is pure power, spirit, and life. 

What could a watery **vapor** <note>original: *vapor*</note> achieve for me there, as the spirit of wine mostly is? And even though the spirit of wine—when it stands in **digestion** <term>digestion: the process of heating a substance gently and steadily to encourage chemical change</term> upon such a tinted **alkali** <term>alcali: a caustic salt used as a medium for extraction</term>—changes in color and becomes very red, it does not follow from this that such a tincture is from the copper. Rather, it is purely and alone from the alkali, from which it draws a subtle **atom** <note>original: *atomos*; used here in the classical sense of the smallest indivisible particles of a substance</note> to itself, and thereby **colors** <note>original: *colorirt*</note> itself. This is something anyone can experience for themselves if they [pour] over such a plain, fiery alkali—which has not been **employed** <note>original: *adhibiret*</note> for such an extraction of the **sulphur of copper** <note>original: *Sulphuris cupri*; the "sulphur" is the active, internal fire and color of the metal</note>—a spi-

# Of the Secret

...poured **spirit of wine** <note>original: *spiritum vini*</note> and set it in **digestion** <term>digestion: the process of exposing a substance to gentle, consistent heat—similar to a stomach's warmth—to extract its essence</term>, it will become just as beautifully colored in this instance as in the other where the few **atoms of the sulfur of copper** <note>original: *atomi des Sulphuris cupri*</note> are present. This must not happen in the way most readers will suspect—that the alkali must be distilled and driven over like a salt, as one does with other **salts** <note>original: *salia*</note> by mixing them with earth, clay, or the like to prevent them from melting <note>original: *den Fluß ihnen zu benehmen*</note>, and thus driving them over into a vapor and spirit with the heavy force of fire. No, such a method of **volatilization** <term>volatilization: the chemical process of turning a solid substance into a vapor or "spirit"</term> does not serve our purpose here. 

For although the alkali can be chased into a volatile spirit in such a way, and such a spirit is of very great power in **medicine** <note>original: *Medicin*</note>—indeed, it far surpasses all other **medicines** <note>original: *Medicamenta*</note>, such as **sweat-inducers** <note>original: *Diaphoretica*</note>, **cutting agents** <term>Incidentia: medicines intended to "cut" through or break up thick humors or phlegm</term>, **dissolvents** <note>original: *Resolventia*</note>, **blockage-removers** <note>original: *Deoppilantia*</note>, **maturing agents** <note>original: *Maturantia*</note>, and **strengthening tonics** <note>original: *Corroborantia*</note> that are found in all apothecaries—as anyone who wishes to prepare and **apply** <note>original: *adhibiren*</note> it upon my **recommendation** will experience with great wonder and apply to the manifold benefit of the sick; nevertheless, it does not serve for the extraction and **essential resolution** <note>original: *essentialen Resolution*</note> of the tincture from copper, because it has become far too weak in the **distillation** <note>original: *Destillation*</note>, in that it [has lost] the greatest...

# Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers <note>original: *Des Sulphuris Philosophorum*</note>

...[it] has left the greatest part of its natural **fieriness** <note>original: *Feurigkeit*</note> with the earth or clay with which it had to be mixed (it being unsuitable to be **distilled** <term>distillation: a process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then condensing the vapor back into a liquid to purify it</term> on its own). Upon this earth, its fieriness has worn itself out and been consumed, as it attacked the earth through its own heat and thus lost its previous power. Consequently, while a very potent spirit for **medicine** <note>original: *Medicin*</note> does indeed pass over from such a distillation, it proves to be quite mild and gentle in taste, and far too weak for our current purpose. 

Rather, in this work, the **alkali** <term>alkali: a caustic, salty substance; in alchemy, it represents a reactive base often derived from plant ashes</term> must be prepared in such a way that it retains its fiery, softening power, and therein becomes even more powerful and penetrating. This must be achieved through its own kind—namely, through another fiery alkali equal to it—but with the condition that this second alkali be **volatile** <term>volatile: a substance that easily evaporates or turns into a "spirit" or vapor</term>. It must be so because it is intended to make the first alkali (which is **fixed** <term>fixed: a substance that is solid and resistant to the transformative power of fire</term>) volatile itself. For if this second agent were not volatile, it certainly could not make the first one so, as no one can give more than he possesses or has within his power. 

Such a volatile alkali must first be produced through the **Art** <note>the "Art" refers to the practice of alchemy</note>, and indeed from the very same source from which the **fixed alkali** <note>original: *fixes Alcali*</note> originated: namely, from an **oily essence** <note>original: *ölichten Wesen*</note>. Since a true alkali can arise from nothing other than the **oily principle** <note>original: *ölichten Principio*</note>, you must therefore hold fast to this oily foundation and strive to transform it into a volatile alkali. If you were to attempt this by the sheer violence of fire, you would not [obtain] a volatile...

# Of the Secret

This way is an unchangeable law (as I have already sufficiently informed you before, insofar as it serves our purpose): that **like delights in its like**, attracts it, unites with it, and transforms it into its own nature. 

If you now wish to turn your **oily essence** <note>original: *ölichtes Wesen*; likely referring to an essential oil or the "sulfur" of a plant</note> into an alkali and transform it therein, following this unchangeable rule of nature, you must mix or unite it with nothing other than an **alkali**. Because it is its nature, property, and essence (since the alkali also originated from an oil), it transforms the oil into its own nature through its fiery power. It turns a subtle, **spiritual** <term>spiritual: a substance that has been refined into a vapor or "spirit" rather than a solid</term>, thin, oily essence into a fiery alkali. 

Yet, because this process happens softly and gently, no violence is done to the **oily principle** <note>original: *ölichten principio*</note>, as the fire does when it forcibly makes the oil into an alkali. For this reason, it remains in its previous **volatility** <term>volatility: the quality of a substance that allows it to evaporate easily or "fly" when heated</term>, and you have thus obtained a **volatile alkali**. ## Of the Secret

...softening essential water, separates the pure soul from the metallic **body** <note>original: *corpore*</note>, and dissolves it in such a way that it is brought back to the **prime matter** <note>original: *ad primam materiam*; the original, undifferentiated state of all substance</note>, and is softened to its innermost center, and has become a penetrating spiritual essence or **quintessence** <term>quintessence: the "fifth essence," the most pure and concentrated form of a substance, beyond the four elements</term>. Not only does this spiritual fire possess such power, but it also has, in and of itself, such great virtues in medicine that one must marvel greatly at its manifold and innumerable powers. For it is ten times more powerful in its effect than the previous **spirit** <term>spirit: in alchemy, a volatile substance or vapor obtained through distillation</term>, which was mixed from the **alkali** with the **lute** <note>original: *Leim*; refers to a sealant or carrier material used to protect glass vessels during high-heat distillation</note> and driven out by strong fire—which itself nevertheless surpassed common medicines in its powers, just as the sun far exceeds the moon with its brilliance.

# Of the Secret

...[than] common fire, so also is a light much more excellent and far more outstanding than the common-consuming light, because its light is infinite and unceasing, since its light follows the nature of its essence. It can infinitely demonstrate such power once shown, even if it were sought out again from the **excrements** <note>original: *Excrementis*</note> and administered anew <note>This refers to the historical concept of the "everlasting pill," often made of antimony or mercury, which was believed to be so pure that it could pass through the body unchanged and be reused.</note>.

# On the Sulphur of the Philosophers

For this [water], even if it absorbs not even the smallest bit of **quicksilver** <note>original: *argento vivo*; common mercury</note> into itself, nor is able to convert it to its own nature, it nevertheless borrows a property—though not the substance—from the quicksilver. Thus, when such water is drunk, it kills all **intestinal worms** <term>lumbricos: specifically large intestinal roundworms</term> and **pinworms** <term>ascarides: small parasitic worms</term>, even those existing in parts of the body where the drink itself never reaches; for the drink is soon entirely carried off into the **urine** <note>original: *lotium*</note>. Indeed, this water becomes more powerful against worms if it has been boiled once with quicksilver. In this way, a single measure of a single quicksilver can "infect" <note>In this context, it means to infuse or impregnate with a quality</note> a thousand measures of water, yet remain in its original weight and property. 

Thus, the **Schools** <note>Referring to the "Scholastic" academic authorities of the 17th century who followed ancient Greek medical theories</note> are forced to learn, even against their will, that certain agents act freely and with tireless strength upon subjects without suffering any **alteration** <note>original: *passionem*; in alchemy, this refers to a substance being "acted upon" or changed by another</note> or reaction, and while always maintaining their same weight. Quicksilver, you see, acts upon the water and impresses its own "character" upon it, yet nothing is taken back from the water in return. 

It is manifest, therefore, that a certain medical power is transferred, changing its natural subject and passing into a foreign object by a single "ray" or "glance," as it were. This happens in such a way that, although the foreign object acquires a power exotic to itself, the agent that inspires this beginning loses or diminishes nothing of its former strength or weight. This occurs without any suffering, lessening, change, weakness, or otherness of the quicksilver. Truly, the example brought forward in this place serves the argument for celebrating the almost infinite virtue of remedies. After this matter had been handled often and in various ways through experience with minerals, it finally taught me what perhaps no mortal before this had yet perceived clearly or from within—

# On the Sulfur of the Philosophers
### Page 95

...[althou]gh filth adheres to the produced waste products, which stir up specific diseases; nevertheless, I consider the disease itself in its entirety, and its remedies, to reside in the **Archeus** <term>Archeum: the vital, internal governing spirit that Jan Baptista van Helmont believed controlled the body's life functions and chemical processes</term>—specifically, whether it is agitated or pacified. Thus, healings are perfected and completed by the slightest touch, vibration, casting forth [of influence], or even by radiation or illumination alone (provided they reach the sensitive life in the **seat of the soul** <note>Van Helmont believed the soul's primary "seat" or office in the body was the stomach and spleen</note>), without regard for the immediate causes of the illness.

And I observe this more powerfully in the sulfurous remedies of minerals: for instance, in the sulfur of **Venus** <note>original: *Veneris*; the alchemical name for copper</note>, of **antimony** <note>original: *stibii*</note>, and especially in the **Sulfur of the Glaura of Augurellus** <note>Referring to Giovanni Aurelio Augurello, an alchemist who wrote the poem *Chrysopoeia*; "Glaura" is a term he used for a purified metallic essence</note>, which **Nymph** <note>A poetic alchemical name for a specific substance or state of matter</note> lacks any other proper name until now. # Of the Sulphur of the Philosophers <note>original: *Sulphuris Philosophorum*. In alchemy, "Sulphur" represents the active, fiery, and soul-like principle of a substance. Here, it likely refers to the "fiery" spiritual insight of the philosophers mentioned.</note>. 101

Referring to the university-trained doctors of the time.</note> did not open themselves to me gradually, nor did their meaning enter into my soul so that I conceived or meditated on one thing after another; namely, that I first considered the Schools <note>The "Schools" refers to the traditional universities that taught the ancient medical theories of Galen and Aristotle.</note> deceived concerning the gathering of elementary mixtures <note>original: *mistionum elementarium*. The old theory that health was a balance of the four elements (earth, air, fire, water).</note>, temperament, complexions, and morbid distemperatures, and that from there I was cast forth concerning the errors of catarrhs <note>original: *catharrorum*. In early medicine, catarrhs were thought to be waste fluids flowing from the brain to the rest of the body, blamed for many illnesses.</note>, and only then at last had I sought the roots, causes, and the very essence <note>original: *quidditatem*. A technical term meaning "the what-ness" or true nature of a thing.</note> of diseases and remedies.

Truly, it was none of these: For if one thing after another had become known to me, I would have considered every such progression to be the inductions of reason and fantasy, which are prone to errors and fallacies. But after the whole [truth] at once—namely, the ignorance of healers, both in the knowledge of causes and diseases as well as remedies and applications—had overshadowed my intellectual conception <note>original: *conceptum intellectualem*. Helmont describes his knowledge as a gift received directly into the mind.</note> with a single flash of light <note>original: *unicâ lucis fulguratione*. This "flash of light" is a key concept for Helmont, representing a moment of divine intellectual intuition that bypasses normal logical study.</note>, I knew without a doubt that this talent was given for the benefit of my neighbors, and therefore it must be presented to the chairs of authority <note>original: *cathedris*. Referring to the professors and officials in the universities.</note>, from whom correction is sought and expected, and this must be brought forth seriously under the threat of punishment.

# Of the Secret

...to remain with copper: The reason I treat mostly of copper in this little treatise <note>original: *Tractätlein*. A common term for a short scholarly or instructional work in the 17th century.</note> and think little of other metallic bodies happens solely because I have resolved to remain with the teachings left behind by our Philosopher <note>likely referring to Jan Baptist van Helmont, mentioned in the preceding pages</note> and primarily to explain them in this little treatise. Since he mentions solely and mostly the Soul of copper <note>original: *Animæ des Kupffers*. In alchemy, the 'soul' or 'anima' refers to the essential spiritual essence or 'tincture' extracted from a substance.</note>, I have followed him in this; otherwise, all metallic bodies <note>original: *metallische Corpora*</note> can be treated, analyzed, and in the same way as reported here concerning copper, their tincture <note>original: *Tinctur*. A concentrated extract or spiritual essence believed to have transformative or medicinal powers.</note> can be obtained. Once this tincture has reached its perfection <note>original: *Perfection*. The state of completion or highest purity in an alchemical process.</note> through proper ripening <note>original: *Zeitigung*. One of the three primary principles of alchemy—Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt—representing the active, fiery, or masculine spirit of a substance.</note> as the tincture from copper.

Therefore, this prescribed method for obtaining the soul from copper can be applied exactly the same way to the decomposition <note>original: *Zerlegung*</note> of other metallic bodies. Thus, it is not necessary to mention anything more about them, as they are all contained within a single process. However, the reader should not think that by the words "metallic bodies" I wish to be understood as referring only to those well-known metals named by everyone, such as gold, lead, tin, iron, etc. Not at all; for if this had been my meaning, I would not have written "metallic bodies" in the title of this chapter, but rather "metals" <note>original: *Metalla*</note>. Rather, by the term metallic subjects <note>original: *metallische Subjecta*</note>, I mean those minerals <note>original: *Mineralia*</note> that possess the same fixity <note>original: *Fixität*. The quality of a substance that allows it to resist the action of fire without evaporating or changing.</note> as the metals; even if they cannot be melted like the other metals, they must nonetheless...

# Of the Secret

once the <term>dissolution</term> <note>original: *solution*</note> has occurred and these two are compared against one another, one will see that the solution of the **bloodstone** <note>original: *Blutstein*; hematite</note> is just as magnificent and beautiful in its golden color as that of the best gold—indeed, it is almost more beautiful to behold, with a more striking golden luster than gold itself. Their great powers prove this to be so, serving as the clearly imprinted seals of benevolent Nature to convince us of it.

# Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers. III

...than this, which I now wish to prove: namely, that from entirely volatile minerals—such as **antimony** <note>original: *Antimonio*</note>, **orpiment** <note>original: *Auripigmento*; a deep orange-yellow arsenic sulfide mineral used as a pigment and in alchemy</note>, **zinc**, and the like—a similar treasure of health can be <term>extracted</term> <note>original: *educiert*</note>. This is because the Sulfur of the Philosophers prepared from volatile minerals is of exactly the same nature, property, power, and virtue as that prepared from fixed metallic bodies. Therefore, I must also mention here how such a gem might also be obtained from these minerals. Now, in order to make this even clearer to the eager seeker of truth, they should first be informed <term>generally</term> <note>original: *in genere*</note> that just as volatile minerals are very different from fixed metallic bodies—in that the former are entirely volatile, entirely "open," and thus very close to the **first essence** <note>original: *ersten Wesen*; the primal, unrefined state of matter</note>, while on the other hand, metallic <term>subjects</term> <note>original: *Subjecta*</note> are very firmly <term>coagulated</term> <note>original: *coagulirt*; thickened or solidified from a liquid state</note>, locked, and

# Of the Mystery
-> (continuation from previous page) <-

...be locked away, and thus these two types of subjects <note>original: *Subjecta*; referring to the base materials or substances being worked upon</note> differ greatly from each other by their innate nature. There is a great distinction between them according to their constitution; likewise, there is a great difference in their preparation. The volatile minerals must not be treated in the same way as the fixed metallic subjects. These fixed metals must first be awakened from death and made alive—as if released from their hard-locked bolts—through powerful double corrosives <term>corrosives</term> <note>original: *corrosiva*; reactive substances, usually strong acids or salts, used to dissolve or "open" a metal</note>. 

Such a process is not necessary for the volatile minerals, because they have not yet entered into such a hard coagulation <term>coagulation</term> <note>original: *Coagulation*; the state of being solidified or fixed into a permanent, non-reactive form</note> and locked death; instead, they remain volatile, alive, and active. Therefore, a different process <note>original: *Proceß*</note> must be undertaken with them. 

Furthermore, minerals are not all of the same kind, and the methods by which their medicinal treasure <note>original: *Artzeneyischer Schatz*; the potent healing essence hidden within raw matter</note> can be obtained vary. Our **Philosopher** <note>Likely Paracelsus, the influential Swiss-German physician and alchemist</note> directs us to different methods for different minerals. For this reason, I will specifically mention one and the other, as far as our Philosopher provides occasion in his writings, and I wish to present each one individually. 

Our Philosopher first recommends to us the **Sulfurs of Minerals** <note>original: *Sulphura Mineralium*; the combustible, essential "soul" of the mineral</note> in general and mixed together. He teaches that after they have been corrected and perfected <note>original: *corrigirt und perficirt*; purified of toxins and brought to their highest energetic state</note>, they then cure all or most diseases. The fact that they can achieve such perfection <note>original: *Perfection*</note> sufficiently indicates that they are just as much the **Sulfur of the Philosophers** as the other me- <meta>catchword: talli [metals]</meta>

# Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers

...metallic bodies, because the Sulfur of the Philosophers is that very substance, and its nature and essence consist in this: that it is a **Panacea** <note>original: *Panacea*; a universal medicine capable of curing all diseases</note>. And I see this more powerfully in the sulfurous remedies of minerals, namely in the **Sulfur of Venus** <note>original: *Sulphur Veneris*; the sulfurous essence extracted from copper</note>, of **Antimony** <note>original: *Stibium*</note>, and especially in the **Sulfur of Glaura** mentioned by **Augurello** <note>original: *Sulphure Glauræ Augurelli*; Giovanni Aurelio Augurello was an Italian alchemist and poet whose work *Chrysopoeia* personified alchemical substances. There are indeed certain sulfurs which, once corrected and perfected, the whole host of diseases obeys, as their many forms are contracted into the unity of the **Archeus** like a clenching fist. ### Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers

...and recommends that the **Word**, specifically and before all others, be added to this **Sulfur** <note>original: *Sulphure*</note>; for this reason, when he recounts the **Secrets of Paracelsus** <note>original: *Arcana Paracelsi*</note>, he placed it at the very top and first of all, with the express declaration that this **Secret** <note>original: *Arcanum*</note> is the greatest among them all, saying:

> First and foremost, the **tincture of lilies** <note>original: *tinctura lili*</note> is reduced from **immature mineral electrum** <note>original: *Electro minerali immaturo*</note> into the **wine of life** <note>original: *vinum vitæ*</note>; one part of it is the **first metal** <note>original: *Metallus primus*</note>, and the other is truly the **essence of the limbs** <note>original: *membrorum essentia*</note>.

The manner, however, of obtaining this high **treasure** <note>original: *Kleinod*</note> and **king** among all **secrets** <note>original: *Arcanen*</note>, he describes thus: that one must first separate the combustible **sulfur** <note>original: *Sulphur*</note>—which gives off a blue flame when burning—from the **mercurial body** <note>original: *corpore mercuriali*</note>. This can be done in the same way I taught concerning **Antimony** <note>original: *Antimonio*</note> in the second chapter; for as long as the **minerals** <note>original: *Mineralia*</note> are volatile, open, and still combustible, there is a process to separate their **sulfur** from them, namely through a **fiery alkali** <note>original: *feuriges Alcali*; likely a caustic salt like potassium carbonate used to break down the mineral structure</note>. Upon such separated **sulfur**, one should pour its (appropriate and solely useful) **corrosive** <note>original: *Corrosiv*</note>—which is nothing other than the **Liquor Alkahest** <note>original: *Liquor Alkahest*; a hypothetical universal solvent in alchemy capable of reducing any body to its primary matter</note>, as all circumstances sufficiently indicate. One must then distill from it and **cohobate** <note>original: *cohobiren*; to repeatedly pour a distilled liquid back onto its residue and re-distill to increase potency</note> it so often until the **sulfur** rises over the **helm** <note>original: *Helm*; the top part of a still or alembic</note> as a **red oil**, etc. Because this cannot be practiced by anyone except a possessor of the **Liquor Alkahest**, we shall simply pass over it and not linger in vain, for whoever possesses such a **liquor** has no need of my **instruction**, but will already know himself how to proceed further according to the guidance of our **author**.

# Of the Mystery

Out of dignity, it is better to call it by clearer names, such as **immature mineral electrum**, the **Father of metals**, or the **first metal** <note>original Latin: *Electrum minerale immaturum*, *Patrem metallorum seu metallum primum*</note>, since such and similar names sufficiently express what it is. Indeed, it would be quite wrong to describe it more clearly, for these names represent its entire nature, essence, origin, and property as clearly as a stamped seal. He who cannot learn it from this is neither chosen by God nor born of Nature to be able to do so; otherwise, he would well understand this language of the **first metal** <note>original Latin: *Metallus primus*</note> and the like—namely, what the mentioned words and names intend to say and express.

After the recognition of this magnificent **Subject** <note>The "Subject" is the raw material used for the alchemical work, here identified as antimony</note> (which **Paracelsus** also calls the **Red Lion**, but **Basil** <note>Basil Valentine, a legendary alchemist and author of *The Triumphal Chariot of Antimony*</note> calls the god **Saturn**), it is further necessary that the **Sulfur** be separated from the **mercurial** part, in the same way as is done with **Antimony**. 

For this "sun-glitter" <note>original: *Sonnen-Glanz*; likely referring to stibnite, the shiny, needle-like ore of antimony</note> is ground very fine and then mixed with a very fiery **alkali**. It is ground together in a mortar for half a quarter-hour, then water is poured over it so that it runs through the filter. Whatever remains in the filtered water is precipitated with an **acid**, and thus the excellent **Sulfur** falls to the bottom. When dried, this sulfur is yellow in color and burns just like any other sulfur, though with a sky-blue flame, as our **Philosopher** <note>Paracelsus</note> reports. The **mercurial** part, however, remains in the [filter] paper; this is dried and kept for other use. Yet I must

# Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers

...mention this as well: that there is another way to separate **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium*</note> from this noble **Sulfur** <note>original: *Sulphure*</note>. Namely, when the mercurial part is elevated <note>vaporized or raised through heat</note> by a proper fire, it rises up alone and leaves its red companion <note>the sulfur</note> below at the bottom. But such a **Method** <note>original: *Modus*</note> does not belong here, because in this manner the **Sulfur** remaining at the bottom becomes considerably fixed <note>made non-volatile or hardened</note> and is changed from its previous combustible nature.

This, however, should not happen in this Work, as the following **Practice** <note>original: *Mechanica*</note> left behind by our **Philosopher** <note>Likely referring to a foundational author like Paracelsus or Basil Valentine</note> demonstrates. For he requires that the **Sulfur of the first metal** <note>original: *Sulphur metalli primi*</note> should be dissolved in a **Cinnamon Oil** <note>original: *Zimmet-Oel*</note>, which cannot be done unless this **Sulfur** remains entirely volatile and combustible; therefore, one must stick to the first **Method** <note>The method described on the previous page involving precipitation</note>. 

Therefore, pour a subtle **Cinnamon Oil** onto the mentioned combustible **Sulfur**, put it into a **vial** <note>original: *phiol*</note>, and let it boil until your oil has become very red and rich with **Tincture** <note>The concentrated essence or medicinal quality extracted from the substance</note>. Pour it off into a **retort** <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distillation</note> and draw your oil over gently <note>distill it</note> as long as it comes over bright and clear. When nothing more will come over with such a mild fire, then let it cool. Thus you have in the retort a thick red **Extract** <note>original: *Extractum*</note>, like clotted blood. Preserve this. Pour the clear oil that came over back into the vial, and if there is not enough in there, add even more of the **Sulfur of the first metal**, and let it boil again for 12 or more hours. Then your oil will again be very red. After cooling, pour this back into the previous retort with the first thick **Extract**, and draw it over, just as before,

### of the **Sulfur of the Philosophers** <note>original: *Sulphuris Philosophorum*</note>

I should also mention here that there is yet another way to separate **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium*</note> from this noble **Sulfur** <note>original: *Sulphure*</note>; namely, where the mercurial part is elevated <note>original: *elevirt*; vaporized or raised through heating</note> by a suitable fire, so that it rises alone and leaves its red companion <note>The red solid residue remaining after the more volatile mercury has vaporized</note> at the bottom. However, such a **method** <note>original: *Modus*</note> does not belong here, because in this manner the **Sulfur** remaining at the bottom becomes fixed <note>original: *figirt*; rendered solid and non-reactive</note> to a considerable degree and is changed from its previous combustible nature. This must not happen for this work, as the following **mechanical instructions** <note>original: *Mechanica*</note> left behind by our Philosopher demonstrate; for he requires that the **Sulfur of the first metal** <note>original: *Sulphur metalli primi*</note> should be dissolved in an **Oil of Cinnamon** <note>original: *Zimmet-Oel*; in alchemy, this often refers to a specific solvent or a highly refined essential oil used as a menstruum</note>. This cannot happen unless this **Sulfur** remains entirely volatile and combustible; therefore, one must stick to the first **method** <note>original: *Modo*</note>.

Therefore, pour a subtle **Oil of Cinnamon** upon the mentioned combustible **Sulfur**, put it into a **vial** <note>original: *phiol*; a glass laboratory flask</note>, and let it boil until your oil has become very red and rich with **Tincture** <note>original: *Tinctur*; the medicinal or transformative essence extracted from the raw material</note>. Pour it off into a **retort** <note>A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</note>, and draw your oil over gently <note>Distill it at a low temperature</note> as long as it comes over bright and clear; and when nothing more will come over with such a gentle fire, then let it cool. You will then have in the **retort** a thick, red **Extract** <note>original: *Extractum*</note>, like curdled blood. Save this; pour the clear oil that passed over back into the **vial**, and if there is not enough in it, add even more of the **Sulfur of the First Metal**, and let it boil again for 12 or more hours. # Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers

...willingly and gladly rises out of the **antimony** <note>original: *Antimonio*</note>, as when one pours its **vinegar** <note>original: *Acetum*</note> upon it, which wants to have absolutely no communion with the **sulfur** <note>original: *Sulphure*</note>, but instead immediately joins itself to the **mercurial** part of the **antimony**. Thus, the **sulfur** is released from its **mercury** <note>original: *Mercurio*</note>, which is why it also rises with quite gentle heat. In color, it is like any other common **sulfur**, and it is distinguished from the common variety in no way—neither in color, smell, taste, volatility, nor combustibility—except that it gives off a more beautiful light when it is burned than the common one does upon its ignition. 

And yet, despite this being so completely similar to the common sort, and having been touched or extracted by nothing that could have improved it—but rather being willingly **dismissed** <note>original: *dimittirt*</note> from its **mercury** because the latter has been saturated with something more agreeable to it—it nonetheless surpasses common **sulfur** so greatly that the difference between their two powers is a cause for wonder. This difference arises solely from their different innate natures; Nature, according to the first seed of these minerals, immediately gave greater glory to the one than to the other. 

Such is also to be seen from the **Mechanical Instructions** <term>Mechanica|A reference to a specific alchemical text or section of a work describing practical operations</term> of our Philosopher, where he teaches that one should extract a **sulfur** from the **antimony** which, in its external form, is not distinguished from common sulfur, except that it appears a little greener. From such a substance, one should make a **cinnabar** <term>Cinnabar|A bright red mineral consisting of mercury sulfide; in alchemy, it represents a union of sulfur and mercury</term> with the **mercury**, and **sublime** <term>Sublime|The process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here as a method of purification and spiritualization</term> it seven times.

# Of the Philosophers' Sulfur

...as was illuminated by the Teutonic Philosopher Jacob Böhme <note>Jacob Böhme (1575–1624) was a German mystic whose complex cosmological writings deeply influenced later alchemy and philosophy.</note> in his book *On the Three Principles* <note>original: *de tribus principiis*</note>, as well as in *The Signature of All Things* <note>original: *de signatura rerum*</note>, and *On the Threefold Life* <note>original: *de triplici vita*</note> and others besides. Thus, it may rightly be called the King of the stars—and indeed is so in truth—according to the nature of their origin and its great preeminence in splendor. In the same way, the metals and minerals have their king and nature which surpasses them all. For this reason, it is called the *prime metal or male* <note>original: *metallus primum sive masculus*</note>, which is to say, the *Father of Metals* <note>original: *Pater metallorum*</note>, by our highly gifted and deeply learned philosopher Van Helmont <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644) was a Flemish chemist and physician who bridged the gap between alchemy and early chemistry.</note>, as well as by Paracelsus <note>Paracelsus (1493–1541) was a Swiss physician who pioneered the use of minerals and chemicals in medicine.</note>. These men recognized through the gift of God—yea, they have seen perfectly with the eyes of their understanding—that this **mineral electrum** <note>original: *Electrum minerale*; in this context, a "perfect" or "universal" mineral substance rather than the simple gold-silver alloy.</note> relates to all others as the sun does to all other stars. Thus, with good right, it can also be called the King of the mineral kingdom, because that kingdom flows from it just as a tree springs from its root. # Of the Mystery

...with its luster, it stands out above the **Sulfur of Antimony** <note>original: *Sulphur Antimonii*; in alchemy, sulfur represents the combustible, active principle of a substance.</note>, which is indeed of such great power that it is to be marveled at, as the cited words of our philosopher have reported. This is because nothing strengthens all the limbs of the body as perfectly as this Royal Tincture. Therefore, by reason of its excellence <note>original: *secundum excellentiam*</note>, it is simply <note>original: *simpliciter*</note> called by both of them the **Secret of the Tincture** <note>original: *Arcanum Tincturæ*</note>, because the tincture of life—specifically and above all others—has been placed by nature into this Royal Subject. 

In this sense, our philosopher also calls it, among the more imperfect things <note>original: *inter imperfectiora*</note>, the highest and most excellent among the wonders of nature, and the greatest and most powerful for a long life. Common <term>Sulfur</term> <note>original: *Sulphur*; here referring to common brimstone, traditionally associated with the "soul" or "inflammable principle" of a substance.</note> and its ripening are not unlike a piece of meat: just as meat has great power to satisfy and strengthen a person, but while it is still raw, it must first be prepared through proper cooking to communicate its power to the human being.

Thus <term>Sulfur</term>, although it possesses great **balsamic power** <note>Refers to "balsam," a substance believed by alchemists to preserve the body from decay and promote healing.</note>, nevertheless also contains much within it that is contrary and entirely harmful to our nature. I myself experienced its toxicity about ten years ago, when I was thrown down by its stinking poison like a man struck and killed; for a good hour, I was deprived of all my internal and external senses. For this reason, one must take away its <term>toxicity</term> <note>original: *virulentiam*</note>, or rather cook it out and ripen it. ### Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers.

The Philosopher says: Under the guardianship of poison or virulence is hidden the vital fire, which most peacefully leads the <term>Archeus</term> <note>original: *Archeum*; the internal governing principle or "vital force" that Paracelsian alchemists believed directed the functions of the body and nature.</note> toward its desired goals. <note>original Latin: *Sub veneni seu verulentiæ custodia abditur ignis vitalis Archeum in scopos desideratos placidissime deducens.*</note> Such ripening and boiling down can happen in exactly the same way as one cooks a piece of meat: namely, solely and alone through heat and the power of fire. And it is not at all necessary—indeed, it is even very harmful—if one tries to do this through foreign corrosive waters <note>Refers to strong mineral acids like nitric or sulfuric acid, which alchemists often used to break down substances.</note>, as the <term>chemical writers</term> <note>original: *Chymischen Scribenten*</note> teach quite incorrectly in their introductory manuals, commentaries, laboratory processes, and various books. For such corrosive things alter and ruin the <term>Sulfur</term> <note>In this context, the "soul" or "combustible principle" of a substance, which the author seeks to purify into a medicine.</note> in such a way that it takes on another nature. For if it is cooked through simple fire without such added corrosives, it retains its natural, balsamic, soothing, and softening moisture, with which it ought to shine forth. The added acidic and corrosive waters take this away from it, for they force themselves into it, making it harsh and entirely different from how it was created by kind Nature. And although they believe they will reach the end and **perfection** <note>original: *Perfection*; the final, highest state of an alchemical substance where it is fully purified and potent.</note> sooner in this way—which indeed does happen—they do not consider the great damage involved: namely, that through such foreign things that are contrary to it, they completely change, distort, and worsen its nature. Therefore, follow Nature, who carries out all her <term>operations</term> <note>original: *Operationes*; the specific laboratory actions or natural processes performed on a substance.</note> simply and plainly, and conversely preserves everything in its innate state, which you [can] clearly recognize

### Of the Secret

You can observe in all metals, even in gold itself—which in its first original birth, regarding its <term>Soul</term> <note>original: *Animam*; the spiritual or essential core of a substance.</note>, was a true combustible <term>Sulfur</term> <note>original: *Sulphur*; in Paracelsian alchemy, Sulfur represents the principle of "combustibility" or the "soul" of a metal.</note>—how Nature has so excellently preserved this Sulfur in its natural fluidity. Even though Nature has brought it to the highest <term>fixity</term> <note>original: *Fixität*; the quality of a substance that allows it to withstand the heat of fire without evaporating or changing.</note>, it has nonetheless retained its original fluidity.

But through what means has Nature accomplished this? Solely and alone through the fire that she holds in her internal womb; she used no <term>corrosive</term> sharp waters <note>Strong acids like nitric or sulfuric acid, which many alchemists used to dissolve metals, often ruining the "balsamic" quality the author seeks.</note> for this, as the crowd of common <term>writers</term> <note>original: *Scribenten*; a dismissive term for authors who write theoretical books without practical experience.</note> teaches nowadays. Instead, she simply and plainly cooked it through heat for so long until it became perfect and <term>fixed</term>.

Similarly, all the bequeathed <term>processes</term> of <term>Paracelsus</term> <note>The Swiss-German physician who revolutionized medicine by using minerals and chemicals.</note> directed toward the <term>Secret of Sulfur</term> <note>original: *Arcanum Sulphuris*</note> are entirely identical to this mentioned way of Nature. Therefore, we must also do the same and follow Nature, carefully considering how she works, rather than inventing such foreign ways through which we work quite differently and contrary to Nature—and thus bring forth entirely different fruits than Nature does.

Therefore, if you wish to bring the naturally fluid, simple, common <term>Sulfur</term> to its <term>perfection</term> according to Nature, place it in a <term>vial</term> <note>original: *Phiol*; a glass laboratory flask.</note> after it has first been <term>sublimated</term> <note>original: *sublimirt*; purified by turning it into vapor and then back into a solid.</note> a few times. Cook it by itself for as long as its nature requires, using such a fire until it no longer rises up or remains combustible, but becomes entirely <term>fixed</term>

# Of the Secret <note>original: *Vom Geheimniß*</note>

...would have [had to wait], if they had not laid it in vinegar.

Now, such a liquid cannot be any acidic **spirits** or **corrosives**, for these have no fellowship with the Sulfur; not only do they fail to soften it, but—as its enemies—they change, reverse, and ruin its innate softening power, and thus rob it of its naturally-born treasure. 

However, this preparation of the Sulfur is directed toward ensuring that it be preserved and improved in its previous natural gift, and not corrupted or turned into a completely foreign, harsh essence that is entirely unlike its origin. We must, therefore, let go of all **acids** <note>original: *Acida*</note> and **corrosives** <note>original: *Corrosiva*</note> if we wish to reach our intended goal and prepare the Sulfur in such a way that it remains in its original nature and quality. 

Such moisture, however, being useful for this purpose, we can obtain nowhere better than from those things which stand in one root with the Sulfur at their foundation, and thus possess the same nature—namely, **fieriness**—within their power. These are the **fixed alkalis** <note>original: *fixen Alcalia*; alkaline substances, such as potash, which do not evaporate when heated.</note> which, by virtue of their fiery-burning and <term>ripening</term> <note>original: *zeitig-machenden*; refers to the process of bringing a substance to maturity or its final state.</note> nature, can prepare the Sulfur in such a way that the...

# Of the Secret

...awakened; as then such configured **Sulfurs** <note>original: *figirten Sulphura*; refers to alchemical sulfur that has been processed and brought into a stable, "figured" or specific form.</note> powerfully accomplish this according to all their properties, which were awakened and obtained through the excellence of the **Art** <term>Art: Often used as a shorthand for Alchemy or the "Hermetic Art."</term>. Yet this should not be understood as if one were permitted to apply <note>original: *adhibiren*</note> nothing else whatsoever except such a **perfected Universal Medicine** <note>original: *perficirte Universal-Medicin*</note>; no, that is not the meaning, since this and similar **Panaceas** <term>Panacea: A legendary universal remedy capable of curing all diseases.</term> look only to the origin of the disease, which always arises from a **Quality** <note>original: *Qualität*</note> or property of darkness. 

Such a medicine **transmutes** <term>Transmute: To fundamentally change the nature or "vibration" of a substance; here, turning the "darkness" of disease into "light."</term> a light-power—such as the aforementioned **Tinctures** <term>Tinctures: Highly concentrated alchemical preparations that "tinge" or transform what they touch.</term> of gold, copper, **antimony** <note>original: *Antimoni*</note>, and others of their kind—and restores the **infected Archeus** <term>Archeus: In Paracelsian medicine, the "inner alchemist" or vital force located in the stomach that governs the body's health and healing.</term> back into such a state as he previously held in a person's health. 

However, if in the meantime such a sickly spirit or dark property has been active or has brought forth something **material** <note>original: *materialisches*</note>, it does not follow that one is forbidden from expelling, consuming, and abolishing such a **product** <note>original: *productum*; here meaning the physical manifestation of the disease</note>, or that one should not also use other useful internal and external means alongside it. Much less are the necessary manual treatments excluded here for **ulcers** <note>original: *ulceribus*</note>, **dislocations** <note>original: *lutationibus*; likely a variation of *luxationibus*, meaning bones out of joint</note>, **fractures** <note>original: *fracturis*</note>, **wounds** <note>original: *vulneribus*</note>, and the like, as anyone who wishes to use even a little common sense can easily see. And thus, even if one possesses such **Universal Medicines**, good supervision, understanding, and caution still should and must be used. # Of the Mystery

...from that point. [I learned] that Reason does not dwell within the true intellect, but outside of it; this is because truth becomes manifest in the intellect through a medium: *Because the truth understood is nothing other than the **conformity** <term>Adæquatio: The scholastic definition of truth (adaequatio rei et intellectus), where the mind "matches" the reality of the object.</term> of the intellect to the things themselves. For the intellect truly knows things as they are, and therefore, in turn, the intellect is verified concerning the things themselves through the things themselves;* Since the being of things is always true in itself, and their essence is truth itself, whenever the intellect is carried above them, it is always directly true. 

But since imagination, or its reasoning, is an oblique <note>The author uses "oblique" to mean indirect or distorted, like looking through a curved lens rather than seeing a thing directly.</note> mode of understanding, proceeding through reasons and **discourse** <term>Discursus: Step-by-step logical reasoning, which moves from one premise to another, rather than grasping the whole truth at once.</term>—and not through the transformation of conformity—therefore, this rational mode is an improper and deceptive intellect. However, the good, the right, the one, and the true always maintain themselves in one and the same way in the intellect, because in the intellect they stand at the point of conformity; while the evil, the crooked, the oblique, the false, and the manifold arise in many ways, through reason in the imaginative part. 

And so I knew for certain that Reason should not be held in such sublime esteem as it has been until now, especially since reason and discourse thrive clearly even in brute animals. For it happens that an old fox is craftier than a younger one, a fact confirmed by the **rational discourse** <note>The author suggests that what we call "reason" is often just memory and experience, which animals also possess.</note> of the remembrance of experience. Indeed, the numerous bees...

### Of the Sulphur of the Philosophers.

[light] <note>The word "lum" is the end of "lumen" (light) from the previous page.</note> has with it in its own nature; for this reason I concluded that the intellect and the things understood, as such, must be or become of the same nature. This must happen without labor or restlessness, but with leisure in its own light, through abstraction, prayer, and the absence of any other created help. And so, Section 50: Whether this light be entirely supernatural, or whether the intellect is ignited in this way by its own nature, I would rather experience it than define it; this one thing at least I know: that it does not happen without **grace** <term>Gratia: In this context, a divine gift or influence that elevates human capability beyond its natural limits.</term>. For this reason, *whether the intellect is transformed, or transforms itself into the image of the thing understood, it surely has need of divine help, and indeed a singular help,* because at least then the soul beholds its own intellect under the form captured in the said light. And in that **mirror** <note>The "mirror of the soul" is a common mystical metaphor where the mind, when purified, reflects the divine or the true essence of things.</note> of itself, it contemplates itself intellectually, without the reflection of "otherness." In this way, it conceives the knowable thing together with all its being and properties, because this light of knowledge does not emanate outside the intellect, but remains reflected within, over the intellect in all truth and perfect certainty, bringing it to perfection. And so, Sections 55, 56, 57: The intelligibility of the thing itself is nothing but the arrival and approach of unity between the intellect and the thing understood, etc. To make this clearer by an example: *the understanding intellect is not different from the thing understood, otherwise than as a direct ray of light is from its own reflection;* therefore, the essence of the thing understood, in the light of the intellect, becomes a spiritual and essential splendor. Indeed, through a migration toward unity, it becomes in some way the intellectual light itself—something that cannot happen to the souls of brute animals. # Of the Mystery

...in a certain way it understands all other things, inasmuch as all other things exist in an intellectual mode within the soul, as in the **image of GOD** <note>original: "DEI imagine." The "Imago Dei" is a central theological concept where the human soul reflects the divine nature.</note>, etc. Therefore, as much as the soul intellectually understands itself, so much does it progress in that most useful knowledge which can be had concerning created things in this life; because in the **light of its own light** it beholds, in a certain way, the properties, essences, effects, differences, distinctions, and defects of all things. Once this knowledge has arrived, all the nobler speculation and assistance of **Reason** <term>Ratio: In this philosophical system, "Reason" is the lower, discursive faculty that uses logic and senses, whereas "Intellectus" (Intellect) is the higher, intuitive faculty that receives divine truth.</term> withers away; just as, conversely, the true intellect within us is suppressed under the whims of Reason.

And **Section 60**: I have sufficiently shown elsewhere that in all of nature, the doctrine of Aristotle is empty and mere trifles; how much less, then, could it stand in the realm of the intellect? For the being and operation of the intellect depend solely upon the soul. Indeed, we Christians are compelled to believe that our intellect is an **immortal spirit**, a light and image of the Almighty. As its origin surpasses nature, so it cannot be adapted to nature's rules, since it possesses a most simple being, and also because it depends immediately, totally, and continually upon its original pattern <note>original: "typo." Referring to the divine archetype or model from which the soul is cast.</note>. 

Furthermore, without a particular **grace** it is unable to understand anything, because the object of the intellect is Truth itself. Therefore, it does not understand with a perfect understanding except by receiving; however, that which it merely receives, it undergoes <term>pati: A technical term in medieval philosophy meaning "to be acted upon" or "passivity." It suggests the intellect is a vessel for divine light rather than a tool the human "works."</term>, rather than acting upon it. For that which comes to the intellect through Grace is not its own property; rather, the intellect "suffers" or undergoes its understanding only in an illuminating way. Indeed, it is more troublesome, more slavish, and more obscure to work at understanding than to undergo it, because by undergoing it, the intellect receives a nobler light freely given...

<meta>catchword: col-</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the soul contains the essences of all things within itself as an image of God, rendering Aristotelian logic "trifling" compared to spiritual intuition. It defines the intellect as an immortal spirit that must passively receive divine grace to achieve true understanding.</summary>
<keywords>Intellect vs Reason, Image of God, Aristotle, Grace, Self-knowledge, Passivity of understanding, Immortal Spirit</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the intellect's relationship with divine light. The previous page established that the soul's true knowledge comes from looking into its own internal light—the image of God—rather than relying on external senses or lower reason. This page develops the concept of how the intellect becomes "perfected" by passively receiving this divine illumination.</meta>

### Of the Philosophers’ Sulfur <note>original: "Sulphuris Philosophorum." In alchemy, Sulfur often represents the active, solar, or soulful principle of a substance, here applied to the spiritual intellect.</note>

...compared. Finally, *since in the act of understanding it always transforms into the form of the thing understood, therefore participating in the boundless light, it is perfected without weariness, trouble, or the labor of understanding; and the light understood shines, by understanding, through the light of the intellect, so that the things themselves seem to speak to us without words, and the intellect penetrates them even when they are closed, just as if they were dissected and opened.* Therefore, the intellect is always perfected by suffering <term>patiendo: Here meaning "undergoing" or "being acted upon." In this context, the intellect is passive and receptive to God's light, rather than active and searching.</term>, and by receiving.

And **Section 63**: 1. I believe, first of all, that nothing reaches the knowledge of truth except faith and the intellect. 2. That all truth flows from a single truth, which is therefore the original one. 3. That all intellect derives from a single and infinite intellect. 4. Just as all light derives from a single light. 5. Therefore, the essence of truth differs in no way from the essence of the intellect. 6. That our own intellect is empty, void, poor, and dark. 7. That all its clarity, nobility, fullness, light, and truth come to it by receiving and being acted upon. 8. That it is ennobled all the more, the more it undergoes the influence of the light which is above all nature.

If the eager seeker of truth follows this instruction and puts it into practice, he will experience that there is no other way to recognize the truth of natural powers than by turning the senses inward into the center of the soul. There, GOD dwells; and if He is called upon with child-like humility for wisdom and understanding, it is impossible that He, as the highest good, would not richly share His gifts with His poor creature...

<meta>catchword: share</meta>

<summary>This page describes the intellect as a receptive faculty that achieves perfection not through hard labor, but by passively receiving the "boundless light" of God. It argues that truth and the intellect share the same essence, and the only way to attain true knowledge is to turn one's senses inward to the soul where God resides.</summary>

<keywords>Intellect, Divine Light, Truth, Receptivity, Soul, Philosophers' Sulfur, Knowledge of God, Internal Senses</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the intellect's perfection through divine light. # Of the Mystery <note>original: "Vom Geheimniß"</note>

...should share, which I heartily wish for myself as well as for the Christian reader, and I wish to direct the reader to the "golden book" *On the Soul of Man*, Chapter 12, by the German philosopher Jacob Böhme <note>Jacob Böhme (1575–1624) was a famous German Christian mystic whose "Forty Questions on the Soul" is likely the work referenced here.</note>, where he will find a further and clearer account of this; as well as no less in his letters, which are in print; and I will close with the final words of our philosopher in the book *On the Stone* <note>original: "de Lithiali." Likely referring to *De Lithiasi*, a work by the physician Paracelsus concerning the formation of "stones" or "tartar" in the body, which he often linked to both physical and spiritual impurities.</note>: *Medicine is a gift from GOD; but He withdraws His gifts from those intent on profit, who do not even once think of the commandment. Be merciful, even as your Father, who is in Heaven, is merciful; from whom every good and luminous gift descends.* <note>The original Latin reads: "Medicina est donum DEI; Hic autem dona sua subtrahit lucro intentis, nec semel cogitantibus de mandato. Estote misericordes, sicut Pater vester, qui est in Coelis, misericors est, à quo omne donum bonum & luminosum descendit."</note> Let this be said to you, you physicians <term>Medici: Doctors or healers.</term>, and take it to heart, so that you may still have a gracious GOD here, and in your dying take with you a good conscience as a treasure, and may rejoice eternally in the wonders of nature you have performed here.

->Psalm 97, 11.<-

## **Light is sown for the righteous, and joy for those who are upright of heart.**

## The following Chemical Writings are available from the publisher of this little treatise:

**Johann Agricola’s** Commentary on Poppius <note>Johann Agricola (1590–1668) was a physician who wrote extensive commentaries on Johann Poppius’s *Chymische Medicin*.</note>, quarto, in German, Nuremberg 1686 — 3 florins, 30 kreuzer.

**Heinrich von Batsdorff’s** *Ariadne's Thread* <note>original: "Filium Ariadnes"</note>, that is, a new Chemical Discourse, octavo, Gotha 1718 — 12 kreuzer.

**Johann Joachim Becher’s** Shorter Chemical Works <note>original: "Opuscula chym. rariora"</note>, octavo, Nuremberg 1719 — 38 kreuzer.

**Steven Blankaart’s** Chemical Theater of the Art of Separation <term>Scheide-Kunst: Literally the "Art of Separation," an early German term for chemistry, referring to the process of separating metals or extracting essences.</term>, octavo, Leipzig 1700 — 1 florin.

By the same author, *The Art of Separation, or Chemistry*, octavo, in the same place 1708 — 12 kreuzer.

**Herman Boerhaave’s** *Institutions and Experiments of Chemistry*, octavo, Paris 1724 — 1 florin, 30 kreuzer. <note>Boerhaave (1668–1738) was one of the most famous European physicians of his time; this work was a foundational textbook for 18th-century chemistry.</note>

**Johann Friedrich Cartheuser’s** *Elements of Medical Chemistry*, octavo, Halle 1736 — 24 kreuzer.

*The Great Mystery* <note>original: "Mysterium magnum"</note>, or the discovered way to prepare the **Philosopher's Stone** <term>Stein der Weisen: The legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and achieving physical and spiritual perfection.</term>, octavo — 8 kreuzer.

**Grabau’s** *Thoughts on the Philosopher's Stone*, octavo — 6 kreuzer.

**Johann de Monte’s** *Harbinger of the Dawn*, octavo, Hamburg 1716 — 8 kreuzer.

*Chemical Lodestar*, octavo, 1717 — 4 kreuzer.

*Universal Medical Sun*, octavo, Hamburg 1706 — 4 kreuzer.

**Pyrophilus’s** *Teachings on the Philosopher's Stone*, quarto, Hamburg 1736 — 24 kreuzer.

*Chemical Sun*, octavo, Frankfurt 1740 — 12 kreuzer.

*Metallic Orchard* <note>original: "Metallischer Baumgarten"</note>, octavo, in the same place 1741 — 8 kreuzer.
